Q: Belgium played the first official international between two independent countries in continental Europe, in 1904. Who were the opponents?
A: France
Q: In the 1970s Belgium briefly switched to all white shirts for evening matches, and got a new nickname. What were they called?
A: The White Devils
Q: In 1982 a Belgian club paid Waterschei to lie down in the title decider, a scandal that broke two years later. Which club?
A: Standard Liege
Q: In August 2015 ADO Den Haag's goalkeeper equalised against PSV in the 95th minute with a leaping backheel. Who was he?
A: Martin Hansen
Q: Bjorn Kuipers refereed the Euro 2020 final. What was his day job back in Oldenzaal?
A: Running supermarkets
Q: Roda JC fans call the team De Koempels. What does that word mean?
A: Mining mates, from the Kerkrade coal pits
Q: In January 2010 a Dutch professional club founded in 1889 went bankrupt in mid-season and was thrown out of the Eerste Divisie. Which club?
A: HFC Haarlem
Q: Which Dutch coach snapped at a journalist with a line that entered the Dutch language, 'Am I so smart, or are you so stupid?'
A: Louis van Gaal
Q: Belgian clubs each carry a matricule, a registration number by seniority. Which club is number 1?
A: Royal Antwerp
Q: Racing Genk were created in 1988 by merging two clubs from the Limburg coalfield. Name either of them.
A: Winterslag or Waterschei Thor
Q: Which Kerkrade born coach, who gave his name to a skills training method, won the 1974 UEFA Cup with Feyenoord?
A: Wiel Coerver
Q: The FA's founding meetings in 1863 banned hacking, kicking opponents in the shins. Which club walked out in protest and went off towards rugby?
A: Blackheath
Q: Herbert Chapman spent years lobbying to get a London Underground station renamed, and won in 1932. Which station?
A: Gillespie Road, which became Arsenal
Q: Juventus gave up their pink shirts in 1903 after a delivery arrived from England. Whose kit did they end up wearing?
A: Notts County's black and white stripes
Q: The Daily Mail called Wolves champions of the world after they beat Honved in 1954. What did the annoyed Frenchman Gabriel Hanot launch in response?
A: The European Cup
Q: Chievo Verona are known as the Flying Donkeys. Who came up with the name?
A: Hellas Verona fans, who jeered that donkeys would fly before Chievo reached Serie A
Q: Which change to the Football League did Jimmy Hill campaign for, adopted in England in 1981?
A: Three points for a win instead of two
Q: Which law arrived for the 1992-93 season, blamed largely on how dull the 1990 World Cup had been?
A: The back-pass rule, keepers could no longer pick up a deliberate kick from a team mate
Q: A riot at Dinamo Zagreb's ground in May 1990 turned their 21 year old captain into a Croatian national symbol. Who was he and what did he do?
A: Zvonimir Boban, who kicked a policeman who was beating a Dinamo fan
Q: Serbia against Albania in Belgrade in 2014 was abandoned after an object appeared over the pitch. What was it?
A: A drone trailing a Greater Albania flag
Q: Alcohol was banned in Scottish football grounds after trouble at which 1980 match?
A: The Scottish Cup final between Celtic and Rangers, which ended in a mass brawl on the Hampden pitch
Q: Saint-Étienne fans still blame one feature of Hampden Park for the 1976 European Cup final. What?
A: The square goalposts, two of their efforts came back off the crossbar
Q: Brian Clough called Poland's goalkeeper a circus clown in gloves before the 1973 Wembley qualifier. What did the 1-1 draw cost England?
A: Their place at the 1974 World Cup
Q: 1. FC Köln's mascot is a live goat called Hennes. Who was he named after?
A: Hennes Weisweiler, the club's player-coach when a circus gave them the goat in 1950
Q: Which Italian club was founded by an Englishman from Nottingham who said their colours would be red like fire and black like the fear they caused?
A: AC Milan, founded by Herbert Kilpin
Q: AS Roma were created in 1927 by merging clubs from the city. Which Rome club refused to be part of it?
A: Lazio
Q: Wales and Juventus forward John Charles played more than 700 games. What did he never once receive?
A: A booking or a sending off
Q: Germans call miserable rainy weather Fritz Walter weather after the 1954 World Cup winning captain. Why did he play better in it?
A: Malaria he caught as a prisoner of war left him unable to handle heat
Q: How did Galatasaray fans greet Manchester United at Istanbul airport in 1993?
A: Banners reading Welcome to Hell
Q: After Scotland beat England 2-1 at Wembley in 1977, what did their fans take home with them?
A: The goalposts, which they snapped, and lumps of the pitch
Q: The first football match under floodlights was played in 1878, before Edison had even patented his light bulb. At which ground?
A: Bramall Lane in Sheffield
Q: The European Championship trophy is named after the Frenchman who pushed for the tournament from the 1920s. What did he never get to see?
A: The tournament itself, Henri Delaunay died in 1955 and the first Euros were in 1960
Q: Italy's oldest club was founded in 1893 at the British consulate and still spells its name the English way. Which club?
A: Genoa, rather than Genova
Q: AEK Athens were founded in 1924 by refugees from one city, and their badge still says so. Which city?
A: Constantinople, hence the Byzantine double-headed eagle
Q: The original FA Cup was stolen from a Birmingham shop window in 1895 and never recovered. Which club had just won it?
A: Aston Villa, who were fined 25 pounds to replace it
Q: On Christmas Day 1937 Charlton's Sam Bartram was still guarding his goal at Stamford Bridge when a policeman found him. Why?
A: Fog, the match had been abandoned a quarter of an hour earlier and nobody had told him
Q: After liquidation in 2012, the new Rangers company was rejected by the Scottish Premier League. Where did they have to start again?
A: The Third Division, the bottom tier, on a 25-5 vote of the league's clubs
Q: Las Palmas trailed Real Madrid 1-3 at the Estadio Insular in March 1986 with eight minutes left. How did it finish?
A: 4-3 to Las Palmas
Q: Las Palmas left the Estadio Insular in 2003. What is on the site now?
A: A public park
Q: Rayo Vallecano's diagonal red sash was copied from a South American club. Which one?
A: River Plate
Q: What is missing from Rayo Vallecano's Estadio de Vallecas that every other top flight ground in Spain has?
A: A fourth stand, it only has three, with flats behind one goal
Q: Villarreal fans got the Yellow Submarine nickname in 1967 by playing a record behind the goal. Whose record?
A: The Beatles
Q: CA Osasuna's name comes from the Basque language, unusually for a La Liga club. What does the word mean?
A: Health
Q: Real Sociedad ran a Basques only policy for decades. Which Liverpool striker broke it in 1989 as their first foreign signing?
A: John Aldridge
Q: In October 2009 third tier Alcorcón hosted Real Madrid in the Copa del Rey. What was the score in the Alcorconazo?
A: 4-0 to Alcorcón
Q: Boca Juniors picked blue and yellow in 1907 by walking to the docks and taking the colours of the next ship's flag. Which country's flag was it?
A: Sweden
Q: Corinthians of São Paulo took their name in 1910 from a touring team the founders had just watched. Where were those tourists from?
A: England, the amateur club Corinthian FC of London
Q: Newell's Old Boys of Rosario are named after Isaac Newell. What was his job?
A: He was an English schoolteacher
Q: Ecuador's biggest club, Barcelona SC of Guayaquil, was founded in 1925. Why the name?
A: The founder Eutimio Pérez was a Spanish immigrant from Barcelona
Q: Fluminense fans throw clouds of white powder in the air and the club is nicknamed after it. What is the powder?
A: Rice powder, pó de arroz
Q: The 2018 Copa Libertadores final between Boca and River was moved out of South America after River fans attacked the Boca bus. Where was it played?
A: Madrid, at the Bernabéu
Q: The 1986 World Cup was awarded to Colombia, who gave it up in 1982 because they could not afford it. Who took it on?
A: Mexico
Q: Chile hosted the 1962 World Cup two years after a national catastrophe. What was it?
A: The 1960 Valdivia earthquake, the strongest ever recorded
Q: Mexican club Cruz Azul was founded in 1927 by the workers of one factory. What did the factory make?
A: Cement
Q: Chivas de Guadalajara have kept one unwritten rule since 1943. What is it?
A: They only field Mexican players
Q: Which country became the first African side to top a World Cup group, in Mexico in 1986?
A: Morocco
Q: Zambia won the 2012 Africa Cup of Nations final in Libreville, close to a place that haunts their football. What happened there in 1993?
A: The plane carrying the national team crashed into the sea, killing everyone on board
Q: Which Saudi player ran most of the length of the pitch to score against Belgium at the 1994 World Cup and put his country into the second round?
A: Saeed Al-Owairan
Q: North Korea were based in one English town for the 1966 World Cup and the locals adopted them completely. Which town?
A: Middlesbrough
Q: What was the score when Iran beat the Maldives in a 1997 World Cup qualifier, still the Maldives' worst defeat?
A: 17-0
Q: Which dictator's son ran Iraqi football and the Olympic committee from 1984, and had players beaten and tortured for losing?
A: Uday Hussein, son of Saddam
Q: UD Las Palmas' mascot Pío-Pío came out of a fan competition in the 1990s. What animal is he?
A: A canary, in the club's yellow
Q: Mexico did not play a single qualifier for the 1990 World Cup. Why not?
A: FIFA banned them for two years after they fielded overage players in a youth tournament
Q: Facing relegation over a capital shortfall in 2014, Eibar sold 50 euro shares online to fans in about 50 countries. What is that called?
A: Crowdfunding
Q: Atlético Madrid could not register players in the summer of 2017, so they bought Vitolo and immediately parked him somewhere for six months. Where?
A: Las Palmas, on loan

A: Czechoslovakia walked off in protest at the referee and were disqualified
Q: The Netherlands took bronze at the 1920 Olympics, but only after losing the medal match itself. Who beat them to silver?
A: Spain
Q: Anderlecht overturned a 2-0 deficit in the 1984 UEFA Cup semi-final, then admitted 13 years later they had bribed the referee. Which club went out?
A: Nottingham Forest
Q: Standard's players bribed Waterschei to hand them the 1982 Belgian title. What was their stated motive for wanting an easy afternoon?
A: To avoid injuries before the Cup Winners' Cup final against Barcelona days later
Q: KV Mechelen followed their 1988 Cup Winners' Cup with the European Super Cup. Which Dutch side, the European champions, did they beat over two legs?
A: PSV
Q: Which Nigerian scored the first ever goal in the rebranded Champions League, in November 1992, and for which club?
A: Daniel Amokachi, for Club Brugge
Q: Two Brussels clubs, Racing White and Daring, merged in 1973 because nobody was turning up. What did the new club win two seasons later?
A: The Belgian title, in 1974-75, as RWD Molenbeek
Q: A Belgian club took FIFA to court after their Moroccan midfielder Abdelmajid Oulmers was injured on international duty in 2004. Which club?
A: Sporting Charleroi
Q: KAA Gent are nicknamed the Buffalos. Where does that come from?
A: Buffalo Bill's Wild West show visiting the city
Q: In 2003 a Belgian club fielded a squad packed with graduates of an Ivorian academy, including Yaya Toure and Emmanuel Eboue. Which club?
A: KSK Beveren
Q: Where exactly was RSC Anderlecht founded in May 1908?
A: In a cafe, the Concordia, in Anderlecht
Q: Which Anderlecht great, known as the White Pele, lined up alongside Pele and Bobby Moore in the film Escape to Victory?
A: Paul Van Himst
Q: Before he was a cyclist, Remco Evenepoel was a midfielder at Anderlecht and PSV. What job did he have in Belgium's youth teams?
A: He was the captain
Q: The first foreigner to win the Belgian Golden Shoe, in 1975 at RWD Molenbeek, later won three straight titles as Anderlecht coach. Who was he?
A: Johan Boskamp
Q: In September 1965 Go Ahead hosted their first European tie in Deventer and lost 6-0. To whom?
A: Celtic
Q: The eagle on the Go Ahead Eagles badge has exactly seven feathers per wing. Why seven?
A: Because seven is a lucky number
Q: Jan Jongbloed became the oldest man ever to play in the Eredivisie, aged 44, in 1985. Which club was he keeping goal for?
A: Go Ahead Eagles
Q: Rinus Michels took over a relegation threatened Ajax in January 1965. His first match finished 9-3. Who were the opponents?
A: MVV Maastricht
Q: MVV players are called De Sterrendragers, the star bearers. Where does the star come from?
A: The five pointed star in the coat of arms of Maastricht
Q: MVV have played European football exactly once, winning their group in 1970. In which competition?
A: The Intertoto Cup
Q: Joop van Daele's goal won Feyenoord the 1970 Intercontinental Cup against Estudiantes. What did the Argentines do to him seconds later?
A: Pulled off his glasses and snapped them in half
Q: Ajax refused to play the 1973 Intercontinental Cup after their bruising 1972 meeting with Independiente. Which club took their place and lost?
A: Juventus
Q: PSV won the 1988 European Cup with a record nobody has matched. What was odd about their run?
A: They won only three matches in the whole campaign, and none from the quarter-finals onwards
Q: De Kuip opened in 1937 with two free hanging tiers and no pillars. Which two stadiums were the models?
A: Highbury and Yankee Stadium
Q: De Kuip nearly did not survive the Second World War. What did the German occupiers want to do with it?
A: Dismantle it for scrap metal
Q: NAC against Feyenoord in October 1979 was abandoned when a linesman was knocked out by a glass ashtray. How were the last 30 minutes finally played?
A: In an empty stadium, in April 1980
Q: The first foreign professional in Dutch football arrived at Heracles in 1957 and was nicknamed the Black Meteor. Who was he?
A: Steve Mokone, from South Africa
Q: Heracles Almelo had to change their spelling in 1906. What were they called when founded in 1903?
A: Hercules
Q: PEC in PEC Zwolle stands for a combination of two 1910 clubs, one named after a prince and one with a motto. What is that motto?
A: Ende Desespereert Nimmer, never despair
Q: Dennis Bergkamp's father named him after Denis Law, but the name gained a letter. Why?
A: The Dutch registrar would not accept Denis, so his father added a second n
Q: To prove he had the nerve to drop anyone, Louis van Gaal did something startling in the Bayern Munich dressing room. What?
A: Dropped his trousers
Q: In 1975 an amateur club from Spakenburg knocked out three professional sides and reached the KNVB Cup semi-final. Which club?
A: IJsselmeervogels
Q: The fastest own goal in Eredivisie history came after nine seconds, in 2006-07. Which club's defender managed it?
A: FC Groningen, through Arnold Kruiswijk
Q: The fastest goal in Eredivisie history took eight seconds. Koos Waslander did it in 1981-82 for which club?
A: NAC
Q: The youngest goalscorer in Eredivisie history was 16 years and 100 days old in 1959-60. Which fishing town club did Wim Kras play for?
A: Volendam
Q: The fastest hat-trick in Eredivisie history took two minutes, scored by Jan Seelen in 1958-59. For which club?
A: Ajax
Q: Ruud Gullit was not born with that surname. What was he registered as?
A: Rudi Dil, his mother's surname
Q: The 1974 UEFA Cup final at De Kuip was wrecked by rioting in the away end. Which visiting manager took the microphone to call his own fans a disgrace?
A: Bill Nicholson of Tottenham
Q: In April 1938 Austria played Germany in Vienna in a match staged to celebrate the Anschluss, and a polite draw was expected. What happened?
A: Austria won 2-0, Sindelar scoring the first
Q: Borussia Mönchengladbach beat Inter 7-1 in the 1971 European Cup and still went out. Why?
A: UEFA annulled the game after Roberto Boninsegna was hit by a can of Coca-Cola, and the replay finished 0-0
Q: Wales reached the 1958 World Cup without winning their qualifying group. How did they get in?
A: Their name came out of a hat to play Israel, whose scheduled opponents had all refused to face them
Q: Hertha BSC took their name in 1892 from something the founder had been on with his father. What?
A: A steamship called Hertha, whose blue and white funnel also gave them their colours
Q: Facing a refused licence in 2004, Union Berlin fans ran a campaign called Bluten für Union. What did it involve?
A: Donating blood and handing the club the money they were paid for it
Q: In 1919 Arsenal were voted into the enlarged First Division without having earned promotion. Which relegated club lost its place to them?
A: Tottenham Hotspur
Q: Seven players were banned for life over a fixed Good Friday match in 1915. Which two clubs did they come from?
A: Manchester United and Liverpool, who had backed a 2-0 United win at 7-1
Q: Which East German international defected in 1979 on a trip to Kaiserslautern and died in a 1983 car crash, watched all along by the Stasi?
A: Lutz Eigendorf, of Dynamo Berlin
Q: FC St. Pauli's skull and crossbones started with one fan in the 1980s. What did he do?
A: Nailed a Jolly Roger to a broomstick and waved it at the Millerntor, he went by Doc Mabuse
Q: French law now bans professional football on one date every year. Which date, and why?
A: 5 May, the anniversary of the 1992 Furiani stand collapse at Bastia
Q: Spartak Moscow's founder was sent to the gulag in 1942. Which secret police chief, who also ran rivals Dynamo, is blamed for it?
A: Lavrentiy Beria, the man he jailed was Nikolai Starostin
Q: At least 66 people died in a crush at Spartak Moscow's UEFA Cup tie in 1982. When was the death toll finally admitted publicly?
A: 1989, seven years later
Q: Berwick Rangers knocked Rangers out of the Scottish Cup in 1967. What happened to the Rangers forwards Jim Forrest and George McLean?
A: They were blamed for it and never played for Rangers again
Q: Manchester City were drawing 2-2 with Liverpool on the last day of 1995-96 when Alan Ball said a draw would do. Who ran out to say it would not?
A: Niall Quinn, who had been substituted and had heard the real scores
Q: Manchester United's heaviest defeat, 11-3 in a 1904 friendly, came against an amateur side whose own rules banned them from entering cups. Which side?
A: Corinthian
Q: Günter Netzer started the 1973 German Cup final on the bench for Borussia Mönchengladbach. How did he end up winning it?
A: He put himself on as a substitute, then scored the winner in extra time
Q: Marseille were stripped of the 1993 French title over the Valenciennes bribery case. Who got it instead?
A: Nobody, Paris Saint-Germain were offered it and refused, so the title was left vacant
Q: England beat Italy 3-2 in the brutal 1934 Battle of Highbury. What record did the England line-up set that day?
A: Seven of the eleven were Arsenal players
Q: Offside was changed in 1925 from three defenders to two, largely to stop one Newcastle full-back. Who?
A: Bill McCracken
Q: The corner kick did not come from London. Which city's football association adopted it first, in 1868?
A: Sheffield, after a proposal by Sheffield Norfolk FC
Q: Timo Konietzka scored the first goal in Bundesliga history in 1963, inside the first minute. Why is there almost no record of it?
A: No cameras were there and the photographers stood behind the other goal, expecting Werder Bremen to score
Q: Who scored ten goals in a single match for Germany in 1912 and then had to flee the country in 1937?
A: Gottfried Fuchs, in a 16-0 win over Russia at the Olympics
Q: Thirty three people were crushed to death at Bolton's Burnden Park in 1946. What was done about the match?
A: It was played to the finish, and ended goalless
Q: Chris Kirkland's father put a bet on with William Hill at 100-1 when Chris was a boy, and collected nearly 10,000 pounds in 2006. What was the bet?
A: That his son would play for England
Q: Europe had an international club competition from 1927, decades before the European Cup. What was it called?
A: The Mitropa Cup, Hugo Meisl's idea
Q: The 1909 Scottish Cup was never awarded to anyone. What went wrong?
A: Both games were drawn, fans rioted over the lack of extra time and the clubs refused to play a third
Q: Fourteen players were arrested over Italy's 1980 Totonero betting scandal. What made the arrests so public?
A: They were taken in at the grounds on a live Serie A matchday, some straight off the pitch
Q: Who is the pipe-smoking sailor on Sampdoria's shirt?
A: Baciccia, the Genoese form of Giovanni Battista
Q: The goalkeeper who saved four penalties in the 1986 European Cup final shootout never played for Steaua again. Why not?
A: A blood clot in his arm that summer ended his top-level career
Q: The Intertoto Cup was set up in 1961 to serve an industry rather than the supporters. Which industry?
A: The football pools, so betting coupons had fixtures through the summer
Q: Accrington Stanley resigned from the Football League in March 1962 with 13 matches still to play, then changed their minds. How quickly?
A: Three days later, but the League held them to the first letter
Q: Bohemians Prague are nicknamed the Kangaroos and carry one on the badge. Why?
A: They were given two live kangaroos after touring Australia in 1927, and handed them to Prague Zoo
Q: France's oldest club plays in sky blue and navy, the colours of two English universities. Which club, and which universities?
A: Le Havre, in the colours of Oxford and Cambridge
Q: In September 1946 England played two different sides both called Ireland within three days. What did Johnny Carey and Bill Gorman manage?
A: They played for both of them
Q: Penarol's founding club of 1891 was named for cricket and for the employer whose staff set it up. Which employer?
A: The Central Uruguay Railway
Q: Guruceta gave Real Madrid a penalty for a foul outside the box at Camp Nou in 1970. What had the crowd covered the pitch with?
A: Seat cushions
Q: Racing Santander's players took the field for a 2014 Copa del Rey tie and refused to kick off. What were they protesting about?
A: Months of unpaid wages, they wanted the board out
Q: After signing John Aldridge in 1989, Real Sociedad ran an odd compromise policy until 2001. What was it?
A: They would sign Basques and foreigners, but no other Spaniards
Q: Barcelona top scorer Quini was taken at gunpoint in March 1981 and the title challenge fell apart. How long was he held?
A: 25 days
Q: Barcelona took the Salvadoran genius Mágico González on a pre-season tour of the United States in 1984. How did it end?
A: Menotti sent him back to Cádiz and the transfer never happened
Q: The touring Basque side of the Civil War settled abroad and entered the local league for 1938-39. Which country was it?
A: Mexico, where they finished second as CD Euzkadi
Q: El Salvador lost 10-1 to Hungary at Elche in 1982 with only 20 men in a 22 man squad. Where had the other two places gone?
A: To government officials who wanted a trip to Europe
Q: UD Las Palmas moved into the Estadio Insular the year they were founded without building it. Which club had opened it in 1944?
A: Marino FC
Q: For its first seven years the Estadio Insular had a pitch no La Liga club would accept today. What was it?
A: Dirt, grass was only laid in 1951
Q: Real Madrid's 1966 European Cup winners are known as the Yé-yé side. Where did the name come from?
A: The Beatles, after four players posed as them for a Marca photo
Q: Racing Club's long title drought was blamed on something Independiente fans were said to have buried under the pitch. What was it?
A: Seven dead cats
Q: Colo-Colo have carried a black band on the crest since 1927. Who is it mourning?
A: Founder David Arellano, who died in Valladolid on the club's Spanish tour
Q: Brazil ditched the white shirt after the Maracanazo and picked the yellow one through a newspaper competition in 1953. Who won it?
A: Aldyr Schlee, a teenager from Rio Grande do Sul
Q: Argentina boasted they had everything while bidding for the 1962 World Cup. What was Chile's Carlos Dittborn's famous reply?
A: Because we have nothing, we will do everything
Q: Carlos Dittborn won Chile the 1962 World Cup and rebuilt the country to hold it, but never saw a match. Why not?
A: He died about a month before it started
Q: Three Estudiantes players were jailed for 30 days after the brutal 1969 Intercontinental Cup second leg against Milan. Who ordered it?
A: Argentina's military ruler, President Juan Carlos Onganía
Q: Chile's keeper Roberto Rojas went down bleeding when a flare landed near him at the Maracanã in 1989. What did the investigation find?
A: The flare had missed him and he had cut himself with a blade
Q: What did Chile lose as punishment for the Roberto Rojas fake injury in 1989?
A: The 1994 World Cup, they were barred from even entering qualifying
Q: Rio's grand clubs told Vasco da Gama in 1924 to drop twelve players because they were black or working class. What did Vasco do?
A: Refused, in a letter known as the Historical Response, and took being shut out of the elite league
Q: Honduras were flown into the 2001 Copa América to replace Argentina, arriving hours before their first game. How far did they get?
A: Third place, beating holders Brazil 2-0 in the quarter-final
Q: Football's deadliest disaster happened at the Estadio Nacional in Lima in 1964, with 328 killed. What set it off?
A: The referee disallowing a late Peru equaliser against Argentina
Q: Carlos Henrique Raposo, known as Kaiser, was signed through the 1980s by Botafogo, Flamengo, Fluminense and Vasco. What did he never do?
A: Play a competitive match, he faked injuries and fitness the whole time
Q: When FIFA banned internationals above 2,500 metres in 2007, Diego Maradona played an exhibition in La Paz in protest. Who led the team against him?
A: Bolivia's president Evo Morales
Q: Palmeiras were called Palestra Itália until the Brazilian government forced a change in 1942. Why?
A: Brazil had joined the Allies and banned names linked to the Axis powers
Q: River Plate blew a 2-0 lead in the 1966 Libertadores final play-off. What did rival fans throw at them days later, giving River a nickname that stuck?
A: A chicken, painted with a red sash
Q: Alcides Ghiggia, who scored the winner in the 1950 Maracanazo, said only three people had ever silenced the Maracanã. Who were the other two?
A: The Pope and Frank Sinatra
Q: René Higuita spent seven months in a Colombian jail in 1993 and missed the World Cup. What had he done?
A: Acted as a go between in a kidnapping and taken money for it, which was illegal
Q: Spain’s oldest club was founded in 1889 by two Scottish doctors working at the Rio Tinto mines. Which club?
A: Recreativo de Huelva
Q: Argentinos Juniors, the club that produced Maradona, grew out of two anarchist teams in 1904. One was named after which martyrs?
A: The Chicago martyrs of the Haymarket affair
Q: Joe Gaetjens scored the goal that beat England at the 1950 World Cup. What happened to him in Haiti in 1964?
A: Duvalier's secret police arrested him and he was never seen again
Q: Haiti's Emmanuel Sanon scored past Dino Zoff at the 1974 World Cup and ended a record that still stands. What record?
A: Zoff had gone over 1,100 minutes of international football without conceding
Q: Ethiopia reached the final of the first Africa Cup of Nations in 1957 without playing a match. How?
A: Four entered, South Africa were thrown out for refusing a mixed race side, so Ethiopia had a bye
Q: Referee Janny Sikazwe blew full time twice too early in Tunisia against Mali at the January 2022 Africa Cup of Nations. What was the given reason?
A: Heatstroke and severe dehydration
Q: Ghana's team are the Black Stars after a symbol borrowed from a Pan-Africanist's shipping line. Whose line was it?
A: Marcus Garvey's Black Star Line
Q: Two countries got to keep an Africa Cup of Nations trophy outright by winning it three times. Ghana in 1978 and who in 2000?
A: Cameroon
Q: Nigeria pulled out of the 1996 Africa Cup of Nations in South Africa on the orders of dictator Sani Abacha. What did it cost them?
A: A ban from the next tournament, in 1998
Q: Uzbekistan beat Bahrain 1-0 in a 2006 World Cup play-off, complained about the referee and had the result annulled. How did that work out?
A: The replay finished 1-1, the second leg 0-0, and Bahrain went through on away goals
Q: The Dutch East Indies played at the 1938 World Cup and hold a record no one has taken off them. What record?
A: The shortest World Cup history of any nation, one match, a 6-0 loss to Hungary
Q: India qualified for the 1950 World Cup and withdrew. Legend says FIFA banned their bare feet, but what reason did the federation give?
A: Team selection disputes and not enough time to prepare
Q: Timor-Leste were banned from the 2023 Asian Cup back in 2017 and had 29 results wiped. What had they been doing?
A: Fielding Brazilian born players on faked Timorese birth documents
Q: Belenenses started a 2021 league game against Benfica with nine players after a Covid outbreak. Why was it abandoned early in the second half?
A: They were down to six on the pitch, below the minimum of seven
